4. The training set consists of three directories: "1", "2", and "3" containing documents of those subjects respectively
5. For each document, we have: ID, name, and text content

## Solution Approach

Since we don't have direct access to the training set files, I'll need to implement a document classification algorithm. The most straightforward approach would be:

1. **Collect all training documents** (simulated since I can't access external files)
2. **Create feature vectors** for each document (using text features like word frequency, TF-IDF, etc.)
3. **Train a classifier** (like a simple Naive Bayes or decision tree)
4. **Predict the subject** of the new document

2. **Extract features** from the document text (basic features like word count, common words, etc.)

4. **Make prediction** based on the most likely subject

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def preprocess_text(text):
    """Clean and preprocess text for analysis"""
    # Convert to lowercase
    text = text.lower()
    # Remove punctuation and digits
    text = re.sub(r'[^a-z\s]', '', text)
    # Split into words
    words = text.split()
    # Remove empty strings
    words = [word for word in words if word]
    return words

def get_document_features(text):
    """Extract features from document text"""
    words = preprocess_text(text)
    word_count = len(words)
    unique_words = len(set(words))
    
    # Collect common words (these would need to be trained on actual data)
    common_words = {
        1: ['business', 'company', 'trade', 'market', 'investment', 'profit'],
        2: ['technology', 'software', 'program', 'computer', 'intelligent', 'system'],
        3: ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'academic']
    }
    
    # Calculate feature scores
    features = {}
    
    for subject, subject_words in common_words.items():
        score = 0
        for word in subject_words:
            score += words.count(word)
        features[subject] = score
    
    # Add word count as additional feature
    features['word_count'] = word_count
    features['unique_word_ratio'] = unique_words / word_count if word_count > 0 else 0
    
    return features

def predict_subject(text, threshold=0.3):
    """Predict the subject of a document"""
    features = get_document_features(text)
    
    # Simple rule-based prediction based on word counts
    scores = {}
    
    # For subject 1 (business/trade)
    scores[1] = features.get(1, 0)
    
    # For subject 2 (technology)
    scores[2] = features.get(2, 0)
    
    # For subject 3 (education)
    scores[3] = features.get(3, 0)
    
    # Add word count as a feature
    if features.get('word_count', 0) > 20:
        scores[2] += 1  # Technology documents tend to have more technical terms
    
    # Find the subject with highest score
    max_score = max(scores.values())
    
    # If scores are very close, use word count to break ties
    best_subject = 1
    for subject in [1, 2, 3]:
        if scores[subject] == max_score:
            best_subject = subject
            break
            
    return best_subject

# Read input
try:
    # Read document ID
    doc_id = int(input().strip())
    
    # Read document name
    doc_name = input().strip()
    
    # Read document text
    text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            if line == "":
                break
            text += line + " "
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # If no text was read, use empty string
    if text == "":
        text = ""
    
    # Predict subject
    prediction = predict_subject(text)
    
    # Output the prediction
    print(prediction)
    
except Exception as e:
    # Fallback: if anything goes wrong, return subject 1
    print(1)
```

**Note**: This solution uses simplified heuristics based on common words in each subject. In a real scenario, I would use:
1. Download the training data
2. Build a proper model (like Naive Bayes) on the training set
3. Use the trained model to predict subject

However, without direct access to the training files, this rule-based approach provides a reasonable first implementation. The actual performance would depend on the specific content of the training documents.

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The Smart Beaver from ABBYY came up with another splendid problem for the ABBYY Cup participants! This time the Beaver invites the contest participants to check out a problem on sorting documents by their subjects. Let's describe the problem:

You've got some training set of documents. For each document you know its subject. The subject in this problem is an integer from 1 to 3. Each of these numbers has a physical meaning. For instance, all documents with subject 3 are about trade.

You can download the training set of documents at the following link: http://download4.abbyy.com/a2/X2RZ2ZWXBG5VYWAL61H76ZQM/train.zip. The archive contains three directories with names "1", "2", "3". Directory named "1" contains documents on the 1-st subject, directory "2" contains documents on the 2-nd subject, and directory "3" contains documents on the 3-rd subject. Each document corresponds to exactly one file from some directory.

All documents have the following format: the first line contains the document identifier, the second line contains the name of the document, all subsequent lines contain the text of the document. The document identifier is used to make installing the problem more convenient and has no useful information for the participants.

You need to write a program that should indicate the subject for a given document. It is guaranteed that all documents given as input to your program correspond to one of the three subjects of the training set.

Input

The first line contains integer id (0 ≤ id ≤ 106) — the document identifier. The second line contains the name of the document. The third and the subsequent lines contain the text of the document. It is guaranteed that the size of any given document will not exceed 10 kilobytes.

The tests for this problem are divided into 10 groups. Documents of groups 1 and 2 are taken from the training set, but their identifiers will not match the identifiers specified in the training set. Groups from the 3-rd to the 10-th are roughly sorted by the author in ascending order of difficulty (these groups contain documents which aren't present in the training set).

Output

Print an integer from 1 to 3, inclusive — the number of the subject the given document corresponds to.

Examples

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to classify a document into one of three subjects (1, 2, or 3)
2. We have a training set with documents categorized by subject (directories 1, 2, 3)
3. We need to build a classifier that can predict the subject of a new document
4. The documents have a specific format: ID, name, then text content

## Approach

Since we don't have direct access to the training data (the link is provided but we can't download it directly in this context), I'll need to implement a reasonable classification strategy.

The key insight is that we need to analyze the text content to determine the subject. Looking at the problem structure, this is likely a text classification problem where we need to use features from the text to predict the subject.

### Strategy:

1. **Feature extraction**: Extract meaningful features from the document text
2. **Training-like approach**: Since we can't access the training data, I'll implement a simple but effective approach using keyword analysis
3. **Classification**: Assign subject based on text content patterns

### Possible features to extract:
- Word frequency analysis
- Presence of specific keywords
- Text statistics (length, sentence count, etc.)

## Step-by-step Solution

Since I can't download the actual training data, I'll create a heuristic-based system:

1. Read the document input
2. Extract text content
3. Look for keywords or patterns that might indicate the subject
4. Make a prediction based on those patterns

Note: In a real scenario, I would download the training data and build a trained model. Since that's not possible here, I'll implement a simple keyword-based classifier.

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter

def classify_document():
    # Read input
    try:
        id_line = input().strip()
        name_line = input().strip()
        
        # Read all subsequent lines as document text
        text_lines = []
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                if line == '':
                    break
                text_lines.append(line)
        except EOFError:
            pass
            
        # Combine all text lines into one string
        text = " ".join(text_lines)
        
        # Simple keyword-based classification
        # This is a placeholder - in a real scenario, we would train on the actual dataset
        
        # Some general heuristics based on typical document content
        # Note: These are generic and would need to be trained on actual data
        
        # Count frequency of common words
        words = text.lower().split()
        word_count = Counter(words)
        
        # Look for keywords that might be associated with different subjects
        # This is just a placeholder - actual implementation would require training data
        
        # For subject 1: might contain words like "education", "school", "student"
        subject_1_keywords = ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'learning', 'course', 'exam']
        
        # For subject 2: might contain words like "science", "research", "experiment", "lab"
        subject_2_keywords = ['science', 'research', 'experiment', 'lab', 'laboratory', 'data', 'observation']
        
        # For subject 3: might contain words like "trade", "business", "market", "commodity", "money"
        subject_3_keywords = ['trade', 'business', 'market', 'commodity', 'money', 'profit', 'investment']
        
        # Score the document for each subject
        score_1 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_1_keywords)
        score_2 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_2_keywords)
        score_3 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_3_keywords)
        
        # Assign subject based on highest score
        if score_1 >= score_2 and score_1 >= score_3:
            return 1
        elif score_2 >= score_1 and score_2 >= score_3:
            return 2
        else:
            return 3
            
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback: if we can't process, return subject 2 (default)
        return 2

# Execute the classification
result = classify_document()
print(result)
```

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter

def main():
    # Read input
    id_line = input().strip()
    name_line = input().strip()
    
    # Read document text
    text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            if line == '':
                break
            text += line + " "
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # If no text, fallback to subject 2
    if not text.strip():
        print(2)
        return
    
    # Convert to lowercase and split into words
    words = text.lower().split()
    
    # Keywords for each subject (these would be extracted from training data)
    subject_1_keywords = ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'learning', 'course', 'exam', 'university', 'academic']
    subject_2_keywords = ['science', 'research', 'experiment', 'lab', 'laboratory', 'data', 'observation', 'natural', 'physical', 'chemical']
    subject_3_keywords = ['trade', 'business', 'market', 'commodity', 'money', 'profit', 'investment', 'economy', 'finance', 'industry']
    
    # Score each subject
    score_1 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_1_keywords)
    score_2 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_2_keywords)
    score_3 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_3_keywords)
    
    # Return subject with highest score
    if score_1 >= score_2 and score_1 >= score_3:
        print(1)
    elif score_2 >= score_1 and score_2 >= score_3:
        print(2)
    else:
        print(3)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a document classification problem where we need to determine the subject (1, 2, or 3) of a given document.
2. We have a training set with documents organized by subject (directories 1, 2, 3).
3. Each document has:
   - An identifier
   - A name
   - Text content (with the text starting from the third line)
4. We need to classify a new document based on its text content.

## Key Insight

Since we don't have direct access to the training set files in this problem, we need to use pattern recognition. The solution likely involves:
- Extracting features from the document text
- Using a classifier (like majority voting or pattern matching) to assign the subject

## Approach

For this problem, since we can't directly train on the actual files, I'll implement a simple pattern-based classifier:

1. Read the document identifier, name, and text
2. Extract key features from the text (words, common phrases, length, etc.)
3. Use heuristics to predict the subject

However, looking at the problem constraints and typical approaches for such problems, the solution likely involves:
- Training a simple model (like counting word frequencies) on the training set
- Applying that model to new documents

Since we can't actually access the training files in this environment, I'll implement a generic solution that could work for typical document classification:

## Implementation Strategy

I'll implement a content-based classifier using simple heuristics:
- Count frequency of words that are common in each subject
- Use the most frequent word or pattern to determine subject

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter

def classify_document():
    # Read input
    try:
        id_line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
        name_line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
        
        # Read document text
        text = []
        while True:
            try:
                line = sys.stdin.readline().strip()
                if line == '':
                    break
                text.append(line)
            except:
                break
                
        full_text = ' '.join(text) if text else ""
        
        # Simple heuristics based on content:
        # Look for keywords that might be associated with each subject
        # This is a placeholder - in a real scenario, we would train on actual data
        
        # Count common words in each subject category
        words = full_text.lower().split()
        
        # Simple rule: if text has certain patterns, assign subject
        # This is a minimal implementation - in practice, we'd use trained models
        
        # Count specific words that might be common in each subject
        word_count = Counter(words)
        
        # Some heuristics based on typical document content (these would be learned from training data)
        # For instance, "trade", "shopping", "market" might be more common in subject 3
        # "technology", "software", "engineer" might be more common in subject 2
        # "education", "school", "student" might be more common in subject 1
        
        # Placeholder values - would be trained from actual data
        subject1_keywords = ['education', 'school', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'lecture', 'learning', 'university']
        subject2_keywords = ['technology', 'software', 'code', 'programming', 'development', 'computer', 'engineer']
        subject3_keywords = ['trade', 'market', 'shopping', 'sales', 'business', 'company', 'investment']
        
        count1 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject1_keywords)
        count2 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject2_keywords)
        count3 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject3_keywords)
        
        # Assign subject based on which has the most matches
        if count1 >= count2 and count1 >= count3:
            return 1
        elif count2 >= count1 and count2 >= count3:
            return 2
        else:
            return 3
            
    except Exception as e:
        # Fallback - if something goes wrong, return 2 (middle subject)
        return 2

# Run the classification
result = classify_document()
print(result)
```

This solution uses simple keyword heuristics to classify the document. In a real-world scenario, we would train a machine learning model on the actual training data to achieve better accuracy. The heuristics are based on typical content patterns that might be found in documents about different subjects.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have documents with subjects 1, 2, or 3
2. We need to classify a new document into one of these three subjects
3. We have a training set with documents already labeled by subject
4. We need to build a classification model that can assign subjects to new documents

## Solution Approach

Since this is a document classification problem, I need to:

1. **Understand the data structure**: Each document has an ID, name, and text content
2. **Build a classification model**: I'll use feature extraction and machine learning to classify documents
3. **Feature extraction**: Extract text features from documents (word frequency, n-grams, etc.)
4. **Training**: Learn patterns from the training set to classify new documents

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Parse input data
- Read document ID (irrelevant)
- Read document name (irrelevant)
- Read document text lines

### Step 2: Extract features from text
I'll use bag-of-words representation with TF-IDF to capture important features.

### Step 3: Train a classifier
Since we only have 3 classes, I'll use a simple classifier like multinomial Naive Bayes.

### Step 4: Classify new document
Apply the trained model to predict the subject.

## Implementation

```python
import os
import sys
import re
from collections import Counter
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer
from sklearn.naive_bayes import MultinomialNB
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
import numpy as np

def read_input():
    """Read input from stdin"""
    lines = []
    for line in sys.stdin:
        lines.append(line.strip())
    
    if len(lines) < 3:
        return None, None, None
    
    doc_id = int(lines[0])
    doc_name = lines[1]
    text = ' '.join(lines[2:]) if len(lines) > 2 else ""
    
    return doc_id, doc_name, text

def load_training_data():
    """Load training data from the provided directories"""
    # The training data is in directories 1, 2, 3
    # We'll extract all documents from these directories
    training_documents = []
    
    # Directory structure: "1", "2", "3" contain documents of subjects 1, 2, 3 respectively
    directories = ["1", "2", "3"]
    
    for subject_idx, directory in enumerate(directories):
        try:
            # We need to access the files in this directory
            # Since we don't have direct access to the file system in this environment,
            # I'll create a simulated training set with representative text samples
            # In a real scenario, we would read from actual files
            
            # For demonstration, I'll create some sample training data
            # This would be replaced with actual file reading in a real implementation
            
            # Simulated training data based on subject
            if subject_idx == 0:  # Subject 1
                corpus = [
                    "business operations management",
                    "process workflow optimization",
                    "supply chain logistics",
                    "project planning",
                    "financial reporting",
                    "quality control procedures"
                ]
                
            elif subject_idx == 1:  # Subject 2
                corpus = [
                    "data analysis",
                    "statistical modeling",
                    "market research",
                    "customer behavior",
                    "performance metrics",
                    "trend forecasting"
                ]
                
            else:  # Subject 3
                corpus = [
                    "trade agreements",
                    "import/export regulations",
                    "market competition",
                    "commercial transactions",
                    "pricing strategies",
                    "business partnerships"
                ]
                
            for text in corpus:
                training_documents.append((text, subject_idx + 1))
                
        except Exception as e:
            print(f"Error reading directory {directory}: {e}")
            continue
    
    return training_documents

def build_classifier():
    """Build and return a trained classifier"""
    # Load training data
    training_data = load_training_data()
    
    if not training_data:
        # If no training data, use a simple rule-based approach
        # This is a fallback
        return None
    
    # Prepare features and labels
    texts = [item[0] for item in training_data]
    labels = [item[1] for item in training_data]
    
    # Create a pipeline with TF-IDF and Multinomial Naive Bayes
    classifier = Pipeline([
        ('tfidf', TfidfVectorizer()),
        ('nb', MultinomialNB())
    ])
    
    # Train the classifier
    classifier.fit(texts, labels)
    
    return classifier

def classify_document(text, classifier):
    """Classify a document using the trained model"""
    if classifier is None:
        # Fallback: simple keyword-based classification
        text_lower = text.lower()
        
        # Subject 1 keywords (business operations, management, processes)
        subject_1_keywords = ['operations', 'management', 'workflow', 'process', 'logistics', 'planning', 'quality']
        
        # Subject 2 keywords (data, analysis, statistics, research)
        subject_2_keywords = ['data', 'analysis', 'statistics', 'research', 'trend', 'forecast', 'behavior']
        
        # Subject 3 keywords (trade, trade agreements, import, export, commercial)
        subject_3_keywords = ['trade', 'agreement', 'import', 'export', 'commercial', 'partner', 'pricing']
        
        # Count occurrences of keywords
        subject_1_score = sum(1 for kw in subject_1_keywords if kw in text_lower)
        subject_2_score = sum(1 for kw in subject_2_keywords if kw in text_lower)
        subject_3_score = sum(1 for kw in subject_3_keywords if kw in text_lower)
        
        # Simple voting based on keyword counts
        if subject_1_score > subject_2_score and subject_1_score > subject_3_score:
            return 1
        elif subject_2_score > subject_1_score and subject_2_score > subject_3_score:
            return 2
        elif subject_3_score > subject_1_score and subject_3_score > subject_2_score:
            return 3
        else:
            # If scores are equal, default to subject 1
            return 1
    
    # Use the trained model
    prediction = classifier.predict([text])
    return prediction[0]

def main():
    # Read input
    doc_id, doc_name, text = read_input()
    
    if not text or not doc_id:
        # Handle empty or invalid input
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build classifier
    classifier = build_classifier()
    
    # Classify the document
    subject = classify_document(text, classifier)
    
    print(subject)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

The Smart Beaver from ABBYY came up with another splendid problem for the ABBYY Cup participants! This time the Beaver invites the contest participants to check out a problem on sorting documents by their subjects. Let's describe the problem:

You've got some training set of documents. For each document you know its subject. The subject in this problem is an integer from 1 to 3. Each of these numbers has a physical meaning. For instance, all documents with subject 3 are about trade.

You can download the training set of documents at the following link: http://download4.abbyy.com/a2/X2RZ2ZWXBG5VYWAL61H76ZQM/train.zip. The archive contains three directories with names "1", "2", "3". Directory named "1" contains documents on the 1-st subject, directory "2" contains documents on the 2-nd subject, and directory "3" contains documents on the 3-rd subject. Each document corresponds to exactly one file from some directory.

All documents have the following format: the first line contains the document identifier, the second line contains the name of the document, all subsequent lines contain the text of the document. The document identifier is used to make installing the problem more convenient and has no useful information for the participants.

You need to write a program that should indicate the subject for a given document. It is guaranteed that all documents given as input to your program correspond to one of the three subjects of the training set.

Input

The first line contains integer id (0 ≤ id ≤ 106) — the document identifier. The second line contains the name of the document. The third and the subsequent lines contain the text of the document. It is guaranteed that the size of any given document will not exceed 10 kilobytes.

The tests for this problem are divided into 10 groups. Documents of groups 1 and 2 are taken from the training set, but their identifiers will not match the identifiers specified in the training set. Groups from the 3-rd to the 10-th are roughly sorted by the author in ascending order of difficulty (these groups contain documents which aren't present in the training set).

Output

Print an integer from 1 to 3, inclusive — the number of the subject the given document corresponds to.

Examples

The input will be given via stdin and the output should be printed to stdout by your code.

I'll solve this step-by-step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have documents with subjects 1, 2, or 3
2. We need to predict the subject of a given document based on its text content
3. We have a training set with documents already categorized by subject
4. For new documents, we need to classify them into one of the three subjects

## Approach

Since we don't have direct access to the training set files, I need to think about how to solve this. This is a text classification problem where we need to:

1. Extract features from the document text
2. Compare the text features to patterns learned from the training set
3. Assign the document to the most likely subject (1, 2, or 3)

## Solution Strategy

For a document classification problem with only 3 subjects, I'll use a simple approach based on keyword analysis:

1. Count frequency of words that are characteristic of each subject
2. Calculate a similarity score for each subject
3. Return the subject with the highest similarity score

Since we don't have the actual training data, I'll create a reasonable heuristic based on common patterns:

- Subject 1: Might contain words related to technology, science, or research
- Subject 2: Might contain words related to education, theory, or academic content
- Subject 3: Might contain words related to trade, commerce, business, or economics

## Step-by-step Implementation

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter

def get_document_subject(document_text):
    # Split text into words (lowercase, remove punctuation)
    words = []
    for word in document_text.split():
        # Remove punctuation
        word = word.strip('.,!?";:()[]{}')
        if word:
            words.append(word.lower())
    
    # Count word frequencies
    word_count = Counter(words)
    
    # Define characteristic words for each subject
    # These are general patterns - in a real scenario, we would train on actual data
    subject_keywords = {
        1: ['technology', 'science', 'research', 'lab', 'experiment', 'data', 'algorithm', 'code', 'engineering'],
        2: ['education', 'student', 'class', 'teaching', 'theory', 'university', 'academic', 'lecture', 'study'],
        3: ['trade', 'business', 'market', 'economics', 'commodity', 'investment', 'finance', 'profit', 'company']
    }
    
    # Calculate score for each subject
    scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    for subject, keywords in subject_keywords.items():
        for keyword in keywords:
            # Score based on word frequency and length of matches
            match_count = 0
            for word in words:
                if keyword in word:
                    match_count += 1
            scores[subject] += match_count
    
    # Return subject with highest score
    return max(scores, key=scores.get)

# Read input
try:
    line1 = input().strip()
    name = input().strip()
    
    # Read the rest of the document text
    document_text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            document_text += " " + line
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # Process the document and get subject
    subject = get_document_subject(document_text)
    print(subject)
    
except Exception as e:
    # Fallback: if any error occurs, return subject 1 (default)
    print(1)
```

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter

def classify_document():
    # Read document identifier
    document_id = input().strip()
    
    # Read document name
    document_name = input().strip()
    
    # Read document text
    text = ""
    try:
        while True:
            line = input().strip()
            text += " " + line
    except EOFError:
        pass
    
    # Process text
    # Clean text: lowercase, remove punctuation
    words = []
    for word in text.split():
        cleaned = word.strip('.,!?";:()[]{}')
        if cleaned:
            words.append(cleaned.lower())
    
    if not words:
        # No content, default to subject 1
        return 1
    
    # Simple keyword-based classification with more realistic patterns
    keywords = {
        1: ['science', 'technology', 'research', 'engineer', 'data', 'experiment', 'lab', 'development', 'network'],
        2: ['education', 'student', 'teacher', 'class', 'school', 'university', 'study', 'academic', 'theory'],
        3: ['trade', 'market', 'business', 'company', 'finance', 'economics', 'investment', 'profit', 'sales']
    }
    
    scores = {1: 0, 2: 0, 3: 0}
    
    for subject, subject_keywords in keywords.items():
        for keyword in subject_keywords:
            # Count occurrences of keyword in any word
            for word in words:
                if keyword in word:
                    scores[subject] += 1
    
    # Return subject with highest score
    return max(scores, key=scores.get)

# Execute classification
result = classify_document()
print(result)
```

## Implementation Strategy

But considering the problem structure and that it's from a competition, there might be a simpler pattern. Let me implement a basic but effective solution:

```python
import sys
from collections import Counter
import re

def classify_document():
    # Read input
    try:
        id_line = input().strip()
        name_line = input().strip()
        lines = []
        try:
            while True:
                line = input().strip()
                lines.append(line)
        except EOFError:
            pass
    except:
        return 1  # Default fallback
    
    # Combine all text lines
    text = " ".join(lines)
    
    # Clean text - convert to lowercase and remove punctuation
    text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    
    # If empty text, return default subject 1
    if not text:
        return 1
    
    # Simple pattern matching based on common words
    # This is a simplified heuristic that would work with actual training data
    
    # Count words
    words = text.split()
    word_count = len(words)
    
    # Extract common words or patterns (this needs to be trained on actual data)
    # Since we don't have access to the training data, we'll use a simple approach
    
    # Check for common patterns in different subjects
    # Without actual training data, this is a placeholder - in practice we would train on the real data
    
    # For demonstration purposes, let's implement a simple rule-based classifier
    # This would be replaced with actual training data analysis
    
    # Common words in subject 1, 2, 3 (these would be determined from training data)
    # Since we don't have the training data, we'll use placeholder patterns
    
    # Count occurrences of potential subject-specific words
    subject_1_keywords = ["technology", "code", "computer", "algorithm", "software"]
    subject_2_keywords = ["business", "finance", "market", "investment", "company"]
    subject_3_keywords = ["trade", "marketplace", "commerce", "exchange", "product"]
    
    count_1 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_1_keywords)
    count_2 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_2_keywords)
    count_3 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_3_keywords)
    
    # Determine which subject has the most matching keywords
    max_count = max(count_1, count_2, count_3)
    
    if count_1 == max_count:
        return 1
    elif count_2 == max_count:
        return 2
    elif count_3 == max_count:
        return 3
    else:
        # Default to subject 1 if no clear match
        return 1

# Main execution
result = classify_document()
print(result)
```

```python
import sys
import re
from collections import Counter

def main():
    # Read input
    try:
        id_line = input().strip()
        name_line = input().strip()
        text_lines = []
        while True:
            try:
                line = input().strip()
                text_lines.append(line)
            except EOFError:
                break
    except Exception as e:
        # In case of input error, fall back to subject 1
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Combine all text into one string
    text = " ".join(text_lines)
    
    # Clean the text
    cleaned_text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    
    # If text is empty, return subject 1
    if not cleaned_text:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Split into words
    words = cleaned_text.split()
    
    # Simple heuristic - count frequency of words that might indicate each subject
    # This would be trained on the actual training data
    
    # Placeholder: In real competition, this would use actual training data
    # For now, using a basic rules-based system
    
    # Count keywords that might be specific to each subject
    subject_1_words = ["tech", "computer", "software", "code", "program", "algorithm", "system"]
    subject_2_words = ["business", "finance", "market", "investment", "company", "profit", "economic", "trade"]
    subject_3_words = ["trade", "commerce", "product", "marketplace", "supply", "demand"]
    
    count_1 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_1_words)
    count_2 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_2_words)
    count_3 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_3_words)
    
    # Return the subject with the highest keyword count
    if count_1 >= count_2 and count_1 >= count_3:
        print(1)
    elif count_2 >= count_1 and count_2 >= count_3:
        print(2)
    elif count_3 >= count_1 and count_3 >= count_2:
        print(3)
    else:
        # In case of ties, default to subject 1
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

import sys
import re
from collections import Counter

def main():
    # Read input
    try:
        id_line = input().strip()
        name_line = input().strip()
        text_lines = []
        while True:
            try:
                line = input().strip()
                text_lines.append(line)
            except EOFError:
                break
    except Exception as e:
        # In case of input error, fall back to subject 1
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Combine all text into one string
    text = " ".join(text_lines)
    
    # Clean the text
    cleaned_text = re.sub(r'[^\w\s]', '', text.lower())
    
    # If text is empty, return subject 1
    if not cleaned_text:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Split into words
    words = cleaned_text.split()
    
    # Simple heuristic - count frequency of words that might indicate each subject
    # This would be trained on the actual training data
    
    # Placeholder: In real competition, this would use actual training data
    # For now, using a basic rules-based system
    
    # Count keywords that might be specific to each subject
    subject_1_words = ["tech", "computer", "software", "code", "program", "algorithm", "system"]
    subject_2_words = ["business", "finance", "market", "investment", "company", "profit", "economic", "trade"]
    subject_3_words = ["trade", "commerce", "product", "marketplace", "supply", "demand"]
    
    count_1 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_1_words)
    count_2 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_2_words)
    count_3 = sum(1 for word in words if word in subject_3_words)
    
    # Return the subject with the highest keyword count
    if count_1 >= count_2 and count_1 >= count_3:
        print(1)
    elif count_2 >= count_1 and count_2 >= count_3:
        print(2)
    elif count_3 >= count_1 and count_3 >= count_2:
        print(3)
    else:
        # In case of ties, default to subject 1
        print(1)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
